Approximation classes for the anisotropic space-time finite element method. An almost characterization.
Pedro Morin, Cornelia Schneider, Nick Schneider

Abstract
We study the approximation of L p L_{p} -functions, p ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ] p\in(0,\infty] , on cylindrical space-time domains Ω T := [ 0 , T ] × Ω \Omega_{T}:=[0,T]\times\Omega with respect to continuous anisotropic space-time finite elements on prismatic meshes. In particular, we propose a suitable refinement technique which creates (locally refined) prismatic meshes with sufficient smoothness and the desired anisotropy, and prove complexity estimates. Furthermore, we define a (quasi-)interpolation operator on this type of meshes and use it to characterize the corresponding approximation classes by showing direct and inverse estimates in terms of anisotropic Besov norms. Key Words: anisotropic Besov spaces; approximation classes; mesh refinement; space-time finite elements; (quasi-)interpolation . MSC2020 Math Subject Classifications: Primary 41A25, 65D05, 65M50; Secondary 65M60.



1
Introduction

The main goal of this article is to characterize functions that can be approximated at a given convergence rate by adaptive space-time anisotropic discretizations with continuous finite elements.
This study provides an insight into the approximation classes for optimal adaptive space-time finite element methods for instationary problems; it is in the spirit of the results presented in
[
BDDP02
,
GM14
]
for the stationary case, and those from
[
AMS23
,
AGMSS25
]
for time-stepping discretizations of instationary problems.
We show that a characterization is possible with respect to anisotropic Besov spaces, in particular, with the variant of those spaces introduced in
[
MSS26
, Sect. 3]
. The characterization is only
‘almost’
possible in terms of these spaces, i.e., requires a notion of generalized anisotropic Besov spaces to derive inverse estimates, as is the case in previous works for stationary problems
[
BDDP02
,
GM14
]
. This is mainly due to the fact that the approximants themselves naturally lack higher order Besov regularity.

Finite element methods based on simultaneous space-time discretizations have gained much attention mainly due to their capacity for parallel implementation and efficiency, and also due to the simplicity for handling singularities via local mesh refinement; numerical experiments also show an excellent performance
[
VW21
,
LS22
,
DVHB23
]
.
A finite element method for the heat equation that computes quasi-optimal approximations with respect to natural norms has been presented in
[
DSS25
]
. Similarly to what is done in
[
DS22
]
, they also incorporate local mesh refinements in space-time using a refinement strategy that guarantees an anisotropy tailored to parabolic problems. Such local refinement involves prismatic meshes where the elements are cartesian products of simplicial elements in space and time intervals. They allow for a simple implementation of anisotropy without producing small or large angles when the space elements are non-degenerate.
In some sense, our work complements the results from
[
DS22
,
DSS25
]
because it characterizes the regularity of the functions that can be optimally approximated with given rates and different polynomial degrees for the finite elements.
Wavelet-based space-time methods have also been used for solving instationary problems (see
[
SS09
]
and the references therein), but here we focus on finite element methods and cite only the references strictly related to our work.

In order to achieve our results, which are suitable for a variety of different time and space scalings we propose a local refinement method for space-time prismatic anisotropic partitions and prove complexity estimates. A similar refinement method is slightly hinted in
[
DS22
]
, and briefly described in
[
DSS25
, Sect. 4.1]
for a parabolic anisotropy.
Our refinement algorithm is thus not only a tool for performing the analysis of space-time finite element methods for instationary problems, but also an essential building block for the implementation of such methods for different problems.

The first approximation theoretic investigation of the stationary, i.e., time-independent, adaptive (isotropic) finite element method was given by Binev, Dahmen, DeVore, and Petrushev
[
BDDP02
]
, where they considered linear FEM, triangular coverings of bounded Lipschitz domains in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and an algorithm based on the newest-vertex bisection as refinement technique. The latter, in particular, preserves conformity and fulfills a complexity estimate as shown by the first three aforementioned authors in
[
BDD04
]
.
The generalization of these investigations to adaptive stationary FEM of arbitrary polynomial order and space dimension was published by Gaspoz and Morin
[
GM14
]
. Here, a generalization of the refinement algorithm from
[
BDD04
]
, based on work by Stevenson
[
Ste08
]
, was used. The latter itself relies on the generalized Maubach-Traxler
[
Mau95
,
Tra97
]
bisection routine for simplices. In particular, this high-dimensional bisection routine avoided potential degeneration of the minimal head angle of the refined simplices. Furthermore, the approach to inverse estimates using generalized (isotropic) Besov spaces was introduced in
[
GM14
]
.

The nonstationary angle of this topic remains largely open due to its considerable complexity. A first step toward its analysis was taken by the authors of this article and the collaborators Actis and Gaspoz
[
AMS23
,
AGMSS25
]
with respect to adaptive time-stepping FEM. A key tool in these investigations was the consideration of Banach space-valued Besov spaces. However, no inverse estimates are provided there. Instead, in
[
MSS26
]
, we turned towards anisotropic space-time FEM and the corresponding anisotropic Besov spaces. There, a prismatic tensor product mesh was used as an initial covering together with an atomic refinement method for prisms which consists of a Maubach-Traxler bisection in the spatial and an appropriate number of classical bisections in the temporal direction. The number of temporal bisections was chosen such that the refinement correctly accounted for the anisotropy of the function, i.e., different smoothness in time and space. Since this method preserves the mesh geometry on the individual level of the prisms, direct estimates for discontinuous anisotropic space-time FEM could be obtained in the end of this article.

The main results of our work can be roughly summarized as follows:

Direct result

If a function belongs to the anisotropic Besov space
B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T})
, then it can be approximated in
L_{p}(\Omega_{T})
by continuous space-time finite elements of order
(r_{1},r_{2})
on (anisotropic) prismatic meshes
\mathcal{P}
with an approximation error of order
(\#\mathcal{P})^{-\frac{1}{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+\frac{d}{s_{2}}}}
.
If
\alpha_{1}>0
and
\alpha_{2}>0
, the same result holds for functions in
B^{\alpha_{1}+s_{1},\alpha_{2}+s_{2}}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T})
, when measuring the error in
B_{p,p}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\Omega_{T})
.

Inverse result.

If a function can be approximated in
L_{p}(\Omega_{T})
by continuous space-time finite elements of order
(r_{1},r_{2})
on prismatic meshes
\mathcal{P}
with an error decay of
(\#\mathcal{P})^{-\frac{1}{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+\frac{d}{s_{2}}}}
, then it belongs to the
generalized Besov space

\widehat{B}^{s_{1}(1-\epsilon),s_{2}(1-\epsilon)}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T})
(for all
0<\epsilon<1
).
If
\alpha_{1}>0
and
\alpha_{2}>0
, and the error is measured in
\hat{B}_{p,p}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\Omega_{T})
, then such an error decay is observed only if the function belongs to
\widehat{B}^{\alpha_{1}+s_{1},\alpha_{2}+s_{2}}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T})
.

A more precise description of our results can be found in
Section

2.2
, where all the assumptions on the coefficients are clearly stated, after some necessary definitions have been established.

In some sense, what we present in this paper is an extension of the results from
[
MSS26
]
to the case of continuous anisotropic space-time FEM. This is not just a simple extension though, several important difficulties arise due to the fact that the basis functions have larger support, and can be very complicated if the meshes are allowed to have arbitrary hanging nodes.
Hence the refinement algorithm is not straightforward, since we will enforce the space meshes to be conforming in space and allow only for 1-irregularities in time (see
Section

3.2
below); a careful analysis of its complexity is thus necessary.

Finally, we point out that the characterization proposed here carries over Leisner’s cube-based result
[
Lei00
]
in the setting of wavelet approximation to finite elements and general domains. Despite the conceptual similarity, the methods employed in this paper are entirely different.

The paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
, in order to be self-contained, we first discuss some preliminary results, mainly from
[
MSS26
]
, that we require throughout the paper. Moreover, we state our main results regarding the almost characterization of approximation classes with respect to adaptive, anisotropic space-time finite elements. Afterwards, in
Section

3
, we present an algorithm based on the above mentioned atomic refinement technique in order to generate locally refined prismatic meshes that are regular enough and suitable for approximation with our continuous anisotropic space-time FEM while simultaneously allowing to prove a complexity result. In
Section

4
we analyze in more detail the lattice geometry of the space-time partitions generated in
Section

3
in order to construct a quasi-interpolation operator from the Lebesgue spaces
L_{p}(\Omega_{T})
,
p\in(0,\infty]
, to the space of space-time finite elements. In particular, we show that the operator satisfies a local stability property and globally fulfills a near-best approximation condition. In
Section

5
, we first introduce the aforementioned generalized anisotropic Besov spaces and study when they coincide with the classical ones. Further, we use this knowledge to show a Sobolev-type embedding theorem in the classical anisotropic Besov scale. Finally, we apply the previously derived results to prove our main results, i.e., direct and inverse estimate results for the approximation of (generalized) Besov functions with continuous anisotropic space-time finite elements.

An interesting finding is that a comparison of the direct estimate result for continuous finite elements and the one for discontinuous ones previously shown in
[
MSS26
, Thm. 1.5]
yields that the involved constants will only differ quantitatively, not qualitatively, i.e., the constants depend on the same parameters, see
5.20
.

2
Preliminaries and main results

In this section, we first briefly introduce the notation and function spaces that will be used throughout the article and are necessary to state our main results, which we do immediately afterwards.

2.1
Preliminaries

2.1.1
General setting

We will always consider
T\in(0,\infty)
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
, a bounded polyhedral Lipschitz domain, and
\Omega_{T}:=[0,T]\times\Omega
. Furthermore, we will call a non-overlapping covering of
\Omega_{T}
by
prisms
of the form
I\times S
,
I
an interval and
S
a
d
-dimensional simplex, a
space-time partition
. For those and
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,\infty)
, we define the quantity
a(\mathcal{P})
which describes the
(maximal) anisotropy of the space-time partition
as in
[
MSS26
, Sect. 1]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle a(\mathcal{P},s_{1},s_{2}):=a(\mathcal{P}):=\max\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\max\left(\frac{|I|}{|S|^{\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}d}}},\frac{|S|^{\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}d}}}{|I|}\right)\right).

Here and in the remainder of this article,
|\cdot|
applied to sets always represents the Lebesgue measure. Whether the
1
-,
d
-, or
(d+1)
-dimensional version is meant, will always be clear from the context. Correspondingly, we adopt the notation

\displaystyle\kappa_{\mathcal{P}}:=\sup\limits_{J\times S\in\mathcal{P}}\kappa_{S}:=\sup\limits_{J\times S\in\mathcal{P}}\frac{\textup{diam}(S)}{\rho_{S}}:=\sup\limits_{J\times S\in\mathcal{P}}\frac{\textup{diam}(S)}{\sup\{\rho\in(0,\infty)\mid B_{\rho}(x_{0})\subset S\text{ for some }x_{0}\in S\}},

which is the
shape-regularity constant of the simplices
in
\mathcal{P}
, in the sense that
\kappa_{\mathcal{P}}\rightarrow\infty
if it contains simplices
S
whose minimum head angle goes to zero. Of course, for any bounded set
E
in any Euclidean space,
\textup{diam}(E)
denotes the diameter of
E
, i.e.,
\textup{diam}(E)=\sup_{x,y\in E}|x-y|
.

On these space-time partitions, we will study approximation with respect to
(dis)continuous anisotropic finite elements
on
\mathcal{P}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{\mathcal{P},\textup{DC}}:=\left\{F:\Omega_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\mid F_{|I\times S}\in\Pi^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{t,\bm{x}}(I\times S),\,I\times S\in\mathcal{P}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{V}^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{\mathcal{P}}:=\left\{F\in\mathbb{V}^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{\mathcal{P},\textup{DC}}\mid F\text{ continuous}\right\},

where
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
and
\Pi^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{t,\bm{x}}
corresponds to the space of
anisotropic polynomials
on
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
of temporal order
r_{1}
and spatial order
r_{2}
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{t,\bm{x}}^{r_{1},r_{2}}(I\times S):=\Pi^{r_{1}}(I)\otimes\Pi^{r_{2}}(S):=\left\{P:I\times S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\>\bigg|\>P(t,\bm{x}):=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r_{1}-1}\sum\limits_{|\alpha|<r_{2}}c_{i,\alpha}t^{i}\bm{x}^{\alpha},\,\,c_{i,\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

(1)

The sum over
\alpha
is understood over the set of multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|\alpha|:=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\dots+\alpha_{d}<r_{2}
and
\bm{x}^{\alpha}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\dots x_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}
.
Of course, this definition can be easily extended to
\Pi^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{t,\bm{x}}(D)
for non-prismatic space-time domains.

2.1.2
Temporal and spatial moduli of smoothness for anisotropic Besov spaces

The version of anisotropic Besov spaces used in this article and the forerunner
[
MSS26
]
is suited to space-time finite element analysis and does not necessarily coincide with previous approaches to those spaces (see
[
MSS26
, Sect. 1]
for more information on their distinctions). It is based on temporal and spatial difference operators

\displaystyle\Delta_{h,t}f(t,\bm{x}):=f(t+h,\bm{x})-f(t,\bm{x}),\quad\,\,\Delta_{h,t}^{r_{1}}=\Delta_{h,t}^{r_{1}-1}\circ\Delta_{h,t}\,\>\text{ for }r_{1}\geq 2,

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bm{h},\bm{x}}f(t,\bm{x}):=f(t,x+\bm{h})-f(t,\bm{x}),\quad\Delta_{\bm{h},\bm{x}}^{r_{2}}=\Delta_{\bm{h},\bm{x}}^{r_{2}-1}\circ\Delta_{\bm{h},\bm{x}}\text{ for }r_{2}\geq 2,

with
h\in\mathbb{R}
and
\bm{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Those can be used to define temporal and spatial moduli of smoothness
\omega_{r_{1},t}(f,J\times R,\cdot)_{p}
,
\omega_{r_{2},\bm{x}}(f,J\times R,\cdot)_{p}:L_{p}(J\times R)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
for given
p\in(0,\infty]
,
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, intervals
J\subset[0,T]
, and Lipschitz domains
R\subset\Omega
, as in the isotropic case, i.e.,

\displaystyle\omega_{r_{1},t}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p}:=\sup\limits_{|h|\leq\delta}\|\Delta_{h,t}^{r_{1}}f\|_{L_{p}(J_{r_{1},h}\times\mathbb{R})}\quad\text{and}\quad\omega_{r_{2},\bm{x}}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p}:=\sup\limits_{|\bm{h}|\leq\delta}\|\Delta_{\bm{h},\bm{x}}^{r_{2}}f\|_{L_{p}(J\times R_{r_{2},\bm{h}})},

where
J_{r_{1},h}:=\{t\in J\mid t+ih\in J\ \ \text{for all}\ \ i=1,\dots,r_{1}\}
,
R_{r_{2},\bm{h}}:=\{\bm{x}\in R\mid x+i\bm{h}\in R\ \ \text{for all}\ \ i=1,\dots,r_{2}\}
, and
L_{p}(J\times R)
is of course the Lebesgue space of real-valued
p
-integrable (for
p<\infty
) and essentially bounded (for
p=\infty
) functions on
J\times R
. Further, there are averaged versions of the above moduli, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{w}_{r_{1},t}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p}:=\left(\frac{1}{2\delta}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta}\|\Delta_{h,t}^{r_{1}}f\|^{p}_{L_{p}\left(J_{r_{1},h}\times R\right)}\,dh\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{w}_{r_{2},\bm{x}}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p}:=\left(\frac{1}{(2\delta)^{d}}\int\limits_{|h|\leq\delta}\|\Delta_{\bm{h},\bm{x}}^{r_{2}}f\|^{p}_{L_{p}\left(J\times R_{r_{2},\bm{h}}\right)}\,d\bm{h}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

for
\delta,p\in(0,\infty)
. For
p=\infty
, they coincide with their corresponding supremum versions. Additionally, one sets
\mathrm{w}_{r_{i},\rm{dir}}(f,J\times R,0)_{p}:=0
for any
(r_{i},\rm{dir})\in\{(r_{1},t),(r_{2},\bm{x})\}
and
p\in(0,\infty]
. In particular, these moduli admit the following properties.

Remark 2.1
.

Let
f\in L_{p}(J\times R)
,
(r_{i},\rm{dir})\in\{(r_{1},t),(r_{2},\bm{x})\}
,
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
k_{i}\leq r_{i}
,
\delta\in[0,\infty)
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and
l\in[0,\infty)
. Then it holds

(i)

\omega_{r_{i},\rm{dir}}(f,J\times R,\cdot)_{p}
is monotonically increasing,

(ii)

\omega_{r_{i},\rm{dir}}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p}^{\min(p,1)}\leq 2^{r_{i}-k_{i}}\omega_{k_{i},\rm{dir}}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p}^{\min(p,1)}
,

(iii)

\omega_{r_{i},\rm{dir}}(f,J\times R,l\delta)_{p}^{\min(p,1)}\leq(l+1)^{r_{i}}\omega_{r_{i},\rm{dir}}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p}^{\min(p,1)}
, and

(iv)

\mathrm{w}_{r_{i},\rm{dir}}(f,\cdot,\delta)_{p}^{p}
is sub-additive.

Proof.

See
[
MSS26
, Rem. 2.4]
.
∎

If readers are interested in learning more about the properties of these moduli, such as the equivalence between the supremum and averaged versions, we refer them to
[
MSS26
, Sect. 2]
. As mentioned before, the moduli allow for the definition of
anisotropic Besov spaces
1
1

1

This approach to anisotropic Besov spaces is different to the one used in
[
Lei00
]
. The latter extends the one given in
[
BIN79
, Ch. 4]
to parameters
p,q\in(0,\infty]
. If and for which choice of parameters and domains these different approaches coincide, is a relevant question for further research.
, i.e.,

\displaystyle B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(J\times R):=\{f\in L_{p}(J\times R)\mid\|f\|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(J\times R)}:=\|f\|_{L_{p}(J\times R)}+|f|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(J\times R)}<\infty\},

where
p,q\in(0,\infty]
,
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,\infty)
,
r_{i}:=\lfloor s_{i}\rfloor+1
,
i=1,2
, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}|f|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(J\times R)}&:=\left(\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\left[\delta^{-s_{1}}\omega_{r_{1},t}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p}\right]^{q}\frac{d\delta}{\delta}+\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\left[\delta^{-s_{2}}\omega_{r_{2},\bm{x}}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p}\right]^{q}\frac{d\delta}{\delta}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},\quad\text{if }q<\infty,\\
\text{and}\quad|f|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,\infty}(J\times R)}&:=\esssup_{\delta\in(0,\infty)}\delta^{-s_{1}}\omega_{r_{1},t}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p}+\delta^{-s_{2}}\omega_{r_{2},\bm{x}}(f,J\times R,\delta)_{p},\quad\text{if }q=\infty,\end{split}

(2)

correspondingly, represent the
anisotropic Besov (quasi-)seminorm
. In particular, as stated in
[
MSS26
, Rem. 3.2]
, it has an equivalent discrete representation, given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}|f|^{*}_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(J\times R)}&:=\left(\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}2^{n\frac{s_{2}}{d}q}\left[\omega_{r_{1},t}\left(f,J\times R,2^{-\frac{ns_{2}}{s_{1}d}}\right)_{p}^{q}+\omega_{r_{2},\bm{x}}\left(f,J\times R,2^{-\frac{n}{d}}\right)_{p}^{q}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},\quad\text{if }q<\infty,\\
\text{and}\quad|f|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,\infty}(J\times R)}&:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}2^{n\frac{s_{2}}{d}}\left[\omega_{r_{1},t}\left(f,J\times R,2^{-\frac{ns_{2}}{s_{1}d}}\right)_{p}+\omega_{r_{2},\bm{x}}\left(f,J\times R,2^{-\frac{n}{d}}\right)_{p}\right],\quad\text{if }q=\infty,\end{split}

(3)

with
|f|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(J\times R)}^{*}\sim_{\,d,p,q,s_{1},s_{2},|J|,\textup{diam}(R)}|f|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(J\times R)}
.
In particular,
\|f\|^{*}_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(J\times R)}:=|f|^{*}_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(J\times R)}+\|f\|_{L_{p}(J\times R)}\sim_{\,p,q,s_{1},s_{2}}\|f\|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(J\times R)}
, which can be shown as in the isotropic case in
[
DL93
, Thm. 10.1(i) together with Eq. (10.5) of Ch. 2.10]
. Furthermore, if we replace the moduli of order
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, by arbitrary
\tilde{r}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
with
\tilde{r}_{i}>r_{i}
in (
2
) or (
3
), then the analogue of
\|\cdot\|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(\Omega_{T})}
or
\|\cdot\|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(\Omega_{T})}^{*}
is an equivalent (quasi-)norm
2
2

2

We will indicate these (quasi-)(semi)norms via superscript, i.e.,
|\cdot|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(\Omega_{T})}^{(\tilde{r}_{1},\tilde{r}_{2})}
,
\|\cdot\|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(\Omega_{T})}^{(\tilde{r}_{1},\tilde{r}_{2})}
,
|\cdot|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(\Omega_{T})}^{*,(\tilde{r}_{1},\tilde{r}_{2})}
, or
\|\cdot\|_{B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(\Omega_{T})}^{*,(\tilde{r}_{1},\tilde{r}_{2})}
.
on
B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(\Omega_{T})
, where the equivalence constants only depend on
d,p,q,s_{1},s_{2},\tilde{r}_{1},\tilde{r}_{2}
, and the Lipschitz properties
\textup{LipProp}(R)
of
R
(in the sense of
[
MSS26
, Def. 1.6]
)
3
3

3

With a little abuse of notation, we will indicate that the Lipschitz properties of
R
only depend on a set of
parameters
via
\textup{LipProp}(R)\sim_{\,parameters}1
.
. This can be obtained using the technique given in
[
DL93
, Thm. 10.1(ii) of Ch. 2.10]
together with the Marchaud inequalities from
[
MSS26
, Thm. 2.9 and Thm. 2.12]
.

In particular, these spaces have proven useful in the context of approximation with anisotropic polynomials and (discontinuous) finite elements, a setting that we further investigate in this article. Again, we refer the interested reader to
[
MSS26
, Sect. 3-4]
.

2.1.3
Approximation classes

The main results of this article can be expressed in terms of approximation classes. Since our refinement methods will differ for different (anisotropy) parameters, we will require a more general definition of those classes than in the isotropic case
[
BDDP02
, Sect. 3]
as well as in
[
GM14
, Sect. 2]
and the time-stepping setting case
[
AMS23
, Sect. 4.1]
, respectively.

Let
X\subset\{f\mid f:D\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}
be a (quasi-)Banach space of real-valued functions on
D\subset\Omega_{T}
and
Y\subset X
be a linear subspace. Then, the
minimal approximation error
of
f
in
Y
with respect to the (quasi-)norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
of
X
, is defined as
E(f,Y,X):=\inf\limits_{F\in Y}\|f-F\|_{X}
. In particular, if
X=L_{p}(D)
, we write
E(f,Y,D)_{p}:=E(f,Y,L_{p}(D))
.

Additionally, let
\mathcal{P}_{0}
be an initial space-time partition and let
\textup{REF}(\mathcal{P}_{0})
denote the set of sub-space-time partitions created from
\mathcal{P}_{0}
with an arbitrary but fixed refinement technique.
Further, let
Y_{\mathcal{P}}\subset X
be a linear subspace for every
\mathcal{P}\in\textup{REF}(\mathcal{P}_{0})
. Then, the
N
-term approximation error of
f
,
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, with respect to
X
and
Y_{\bullet}
is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{N}(f,X,Y_{\bullet},\textup{REF}):=\inf\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\textup{REF}(\mathcal{P}_{0}),\\
\#\mathcal{P}-\#\mathcal{P}_{0}\leq N\end{subarray}}E(f,Y_{\mathcal{P}},X)=\inf\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}\in\textup{REF}(\mathcal{P}_{0}),\\
\#\mathcal{P}-\#\mathcal{P}_{0}\leq N\end{subarray}}\inf\limits_{F\in Y_{\mathcal{P}}}\|f-F\|_{X}.

Now,
f
lies in the
approximation class

\mathbb{A}_{s,q}(X,Y_{\bullet},\textup{REF})
with respect to
s\in(0,\infty)
and
q\in(0,\infty]
, if and only if

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathbb{A}_{s,q}(X,Y_{\bullet},\textup{REF})}:=\|f\|_{X}+|f|_{\mathbb{A}_{s,q}(X,Y_{\bullet},\textup{REF})}:=\|f\|_{X}+\|(N^{s}\sigma_{N}(f,X,Y_{\bullet},\textup{REF}))_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\|_{\ell^{q}(\mathbb{N}_{0})}<\infty,

(4)

i.e.,
f\in X
and
(N^{s}\sigma_{N}(f,X,Y_{\bullet},\textup{REF}))_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\ell^{q}(\mathbb{N}_{0})
.
4
4

4

For given
q\in(0,\infty)
, the sequence space
\ell^{q}(\mathbb{N}_{0})
consists of all the real-valued sequences
a:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for which the (quasi-)norm
\|(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\|_{\ell^{q}(\mathbb{N}_{0})}:=\left(\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}|a_{n}|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
is finite. The case
q=\infty
works with the usual modification. It is well known, that
\ell^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{N}_{0})\hookrightarrow\ell^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{N}_{0})
, if
q_{1}\leq q_{2}
.

\mathbb{A}_{s,q}(X,Y_{\bullet},\textup{REF})
is a (quasi-)Banach space with respect to the (quasi-)norm from (
4
).

2.2
Main results

We assume that the initial space-time partition
\mathcal{P}_{0}
has tensor product structure and fulfills some labeling conditions explained in more detail in the beginning of
Section

3
. For its refinement, we employ the atomic refinement method
\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}
from
[
MSS26
, Sect. 4]
which, for the sake of completeness, we present in
Section

3.1
. Our first main result is the following embedding of anisotropic Besov spaces into the corresponding approximation classes.

Here and in the sequel, we employ
\mathbb{R}^{+}:=(0,\infty)
as well as
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}:=[0,\infty)
. Further, for
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}),(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
R\in\{\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+},\mathbb{R}\}
, we will denote
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in R(s_{1},s_{2})
if there is a
\lambda\in R
such that
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\lambda(s_{1},s_{2})
.

Main Result 1

(Direct estimates)
.

Let
p,q\in(0,\infty]
,
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,\infty)
with
\frac{1}{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+\frac{d}{s_{2}}}-\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{p}>0
,
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}(s_{1},s_{2})
with
\alpha_{i}<1+\frac{1}{p}
,
i=1,2
, and
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
with
r_{i}>\alpha_{i}+s_{i}
,
i=1,2
. Then the embeddings

\displaystyle B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\frac{1}{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+\frac{d}{s_{2}}},\infty}\Big(L_{p}(\Omega_{T}),\mathbb{V}^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{\bullet},\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\cdot,\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})\Big),\quad\text{if}\quad(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(0,0),\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle B^{\alpha_{1}+s_{1},\alpha_{2}+s_{2}}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\frac{1}{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+\frac{d}{s_{2}}},\infty}\Big(B_{p,p}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\Omega_{T}),\mathbb{V}^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{\bullet},\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\cdot,\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})\Big),\quad\text{if}\quad(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\neq(0,0),

are continuous, where the embedding constant only depends on the parameters, the dimension and measure of
\Omega_{T}
, the Lipschitz properties of
\Omega
, as well as the size and mesh geometry of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.

Remark 2.2
.

For
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(0,0)
, this essentially means that, for the above choice of parameters, it is possible to approximate a function in
B^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T})
in
L_{p}(\Omega_{T})
by space-time finite elements of order
(r_{1},r_{2})
with a decay rate of the approximation error of
(\#\mathcal{P})^{-\frac{1}{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+\frac{d}{s_{2}}}}
on a prismatic mesh
\mathcal{P}
if the refinement algorithm
\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\cdot,\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
is used.
If
\alpha_{1}>0
and
\alpha_{2}>0
, the same result holds for functions in
B^{\alpha_{1}+s_{1},\alpha_{2}+s_{2}}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T})
, when measuring the error in
B_{p,p}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(\Omega_{T})
.

In order to prove a corresponding result in the opposite direction, we require the notion of
generalized anisotropic Besov spaces

\widehat{B}^{s_{1},s_{2}}_{p,q}(\Omega_{T})
,
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, which will be introduced in
Section

5.1
and are defined with respect to a multiscale decomposition. These spaces partially coincide with the classical anisotropic spaces from
Section

2.1.2
which is proven in
5.13
.

Main Result 2

(Inverse estimates)
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty]
,
q\in(0,\infty)
,
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,\infty)
,
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}),(s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}(s_{1},s_{2})
with
0<s_{i}^{\prime}<s_{i}
,
i=1,2
,
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
, and
\frac{1}{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+\frac{d}{s_{2}}}+\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}=0
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{\frac{1}{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+\frac{d}{s_{2}}},q}\Big(L_{p}(\Omega_{T}),\mathbb{V}^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{\bullet},\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\cdot,\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})\Big)\hookrightarrow\widehat{B}^{s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T}),\quad\text{if}\quad(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(0,0),\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{\frac{1}{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+\frac{d}{s_{2}}},q}\Big(\widehat{B}^{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}_{p,p}(\Omega_{T}),\mathbb{V}^{r_{1},r_{2}}_{\bullet},\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\cdot,\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})\Big)\hookrightarrow\widehat{B}^{\alpha_{1}+s_{1},\alpha_{2}+s_{2}}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T}),\quad\text{if}\quad(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\neq(0,0),

hold continuously with an embedding constant than only depends on the involved parameters, the dimension, as well as the initial mesh geometry and size.

Remark 2.3
.

This result is the opposite to
1
. If
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(0,0)
is considered, it shows that a function, which can be approximated in the
L_{p}(\Omega_{T})
-(quasi-)norm on prismatic meshes
\mathcal{P}
created by iterative applications of the method
\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\cdot,\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
, with an error decay of
(\#\mathcal{P})^{-\frac{1}{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+\frac{d}{s_{2}}}}
, has to posses a corresponding (generalized) anisotropic Besov regularity, i.e., lie in the space
\widehat{B}^{s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}}_{q,q}(\Omega_{T})
; similarly for the case
\alpha_{1}>0
and
\alpha_{2}>0
.

The corresponding theorems which prove
1
as well as
2
reveal more detail and can be found in
5.19
and
5.35
, respectively.

3
Space-time partition and mesh refinement

Our initial covering will be given by a space-time partition
\mathcal{P}_{0}
of
\Omega_{T}
, which has tensor product structure, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}_{0}:=\mathcal{I}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{0}:=\{I_{0}\times S_{0}\mid I_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0},\ S_{0}\in\mathcal{T}_{0}\}
. Hereby,
\mathcal{I}_{0}
is a disjoint partition of
[0,T]
given by
\mathcal{I}_{0}:=\{[t_{i},t_{i+1})\mid i=0,\dots,N-2\}\cup\{[t_{N-1},t_{N}]\}
,
qwith
0=t_{0}<t_{1}\dots<t_{N}=T
, and
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is a non-overlapping, simplicial, and conforming
5
5

5

A simplicial triangulation
\mathcal{T}
of
\Omega
is called
conforming
, if for any
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
, either
S=S^{\prime}
,
S\cap S^{\prime}=\emptyset
, or
S\cap S^{\prime}
is an
r
-dimensional common face of
S

and

S^{\prime}
with
r\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
.
triangulation of
\Omega
, where we assume its elements to be closed.

Since we want to iteratively refine prisms
I\times S
, i.e.,
I\subset[0,T]
an interval, and
S\subset\Omega
a
d
-dimensional simplex, by the aforementioned method
\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}
(see
Section

3.1
below), we need to introduce the
d
-dimensional bisection routine, initially proposed by Maubach
[
Mau95
]
and Traxler
[
Tra97
]
,
\textup{BISECT}(d,\cdot)
for simplices which extends the classical, one-dimensional bisection routine
\textup{BISECT}(1,\cdot)
for intervals. The interested reader may find a good summary of the method in
[
Ste08
, Sect. 2 and the beginning of Sect. 4]
. For the method to work, the simplices have to be well-labeled, which is why we additionally assume
\mathcal{T}_{0}
to be a
well-labeled
triangulation in the sense of
[
DGS25
]
or
[
Ste08
, cond. b) in Sect. 4]
.

For
(\dim,R)\in\{(1,I),(d,S)\}
, we will denote the set of created intervals or simplices (via bisection) by
\textup{BISECT}(\dim,R)
, correspondingly. Iteratively, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we will denote
R^{\prime\prime}\in\textup{BISECT}(\dim,R)^{\otimes n}
, if
R^{\prime\prime}\in\textup{BISECT}(\dim,R^{\prime})
for some
R^{\prime}\in\textup{BISECT}(\dim,R)^{\otimes(n-1)}
, whereas
\textup{BISECT}(\dim,R)^{\otimes 0}:=\{R\}
. Further, we will call
R
of
level

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and write
\ell(R)=n
, if there exists some
R_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
or
R_{0}\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
, respectively, such that
R_{0}\in\textup{BISECT}(\dim,R)^{\otimes n}
.

3.1
Atomic refinement of prisms

We will use the refinement technique
\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
from
[
MSS26
, Sect. 4]
with respect to
d
and given parameters
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,\infty)
iteratively in order to create refined space-time partitions
\mathcal{P}
, starting with the tensor product mesh
\mathcal{P}_{0}
. Moreover, we apply the notation for
levels
of elements of a space-time partition given there, which extends the one given above for
BISECT
to
\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
. In order to be self-contained, we include this algorithm for the atomic refinement of prisms
I\times S
here.

Algorithm 1

\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(I\times S,d,s_{1},s_{2})

n\leftarrow\ell(S)+1

m\leftarrow\left\lceil\frac{ns_{2}}{s_{1}d}\right\rceil-\left\lceil\frac{(n-1)s_{2}}{s_{1}d}\right\rceil

S_{children}\leftarrow\textup{BISECT}(d,S)

I_{children}\leftarrow\textup{BISECT}(1,I)\phantom{}^{\otimes m}

return

I_{children}\times S_{children}

\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}
for
d=2
and
s_{2}=4s_{1}=2s_{1}d
.

Remark 3.1
.

(i)

This technique has some nice properties, namely that for such
\mathcal{P}
, it holds
\kappa_{\mathcal{P}}\lesssim_{\,d}\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}
(due to the fact that the spatial
\textup{BISECT}(d,\cdot)
method used bounds the minimum head angle of the spatial simplices from below
6
6

6

In
[
DGS25
, App. A]
, an explicit bound has been established for a parameter related to
\kappa_{\mathcal{P}}
.
) and
a(\mathcal{P})\lesssim a(\mathcal{P}_{0})
as shown in
[
MSS26
, Lem. 4.1]
. This is relevant, since
\kappa_{\mathcal{P}}
and
a(\mathcal{P})
are incorporated into the inequality constants of Whitney’s inequality for approximation with discontinuous anisotropic finite elements and the corresponding direct estimate result, see
[
MSS26
, Thm. 1.2 and Thm. 1.5]
. Generalizations of this result will be proven and employed in this article, which also require a control of mentioned constants.

(ii)

Further, we assume that
\textup{BISECT}(1,\cdot)
splits half-open intervals in two half-open intervals and closed intervals in one half-open interval and one closed interval. This is to guarantee that any temporal partition of
[0,T]
created from
\mathcal{I}_{0}
by iterative applications of
\textup{BISECT}(1,\cdot)
again only contains pairwise disjoint elements. This guarantees that for
t\in[0,T]
, the
active spatial triangulation at time
t
, given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(t,\mathcal{P}):=\{S\mid I\times S\in\mathcal{P},\ t\in I\}

is well-defined and indeed a non-overlapping triangulation of
\Omega
. Similarly,
\textup{BISECT}(d,S)
shall denote a set of two closed
d
-dimensional simplices if
S
is a
d
-dimensional closed simplex.

(iii)

We finally observe that always,
0\leq m\leq\lceil\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}d}\rceil+2
.

In the next lemma we see that the size of an element can be estimated by its level and vice-versa, which corresponds to a similar result for the
BISECT
method given in the beginning of
[
Ste08
, Sect. 4]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{I}{S}
be a
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{1}{d}
-dimensional simplex derived from an element
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{I_0}{S_0}\in\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{\calI_0}{\calT_0}
by finitely many applications of the method
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{\textup{BISECT}(1,\cdot)}{\textup{BISECT}(d,\cdot)}
. Then

\displaystyle\textup{diam}(I)\sim_{\,\mu_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{0}),\mu_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{0})}2^{-\ell(I)}\quad\text{and}\quad\textup{diam}(S)\sim_{\,d,\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu_{3}(\mathcal{P}_{0}),\mu_{4}(\mathcal{P}_{0})}2^{-\frac{\ell(S)}{d}},

where we have used

\displaystyle\mu_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{0}):=\max\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}|I|,\quad\mu_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{0}):=\min\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}|I|,\quad\mu_{3}(\mathcal{P}_{0}):=\max\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}|S|,\quad\text{and}\quad\mu_{4}(\mathcal{P}_{0}):=\min\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}|S|.

Remark 3.3
.

From now on, we will further use the abbreviations

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(\mathcal{P}_{0}):=\{\mu_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{0}),\mu_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{0})\},\quad\mu_{\bm{x}}(\mathcal{P}_{0}):=\{\mu_{3}(\mathcal{P}_{0}),\mu_{4}(\mathcal{P}_{0})\},\quad\text{and}\quad\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0}):=\{\mu_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{0}),\mu_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{0}),\mu_{3}(\mathcal{P}_{0}),\mu_{4}(\mathcal{P}_{0})\}.

Proof.

For the temporal part, this can directly be seen by
\textup{diam}(I)=|I|=2^{-\ell(I)}|I_{0}|\sim_{\,\mu_{t}(\mathcal{P}_{0})}2^{-\ell(I)}
. Since, the
\textup{BISECT}(d,\cdot)
method controls the minimum head angle of the simplices, we first have
\textup{diam}(S)\sim_{\,d,\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}}|S|^{\frac{1}{d}}
. Together with
[
Ste08
, Eq. (4.1)]
and its surrounding text, we can derive the existence of two positive constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that

\displaystyle C_{1}\,2^{-\frac{\ell(S)}{d}}\leq|S|^{\frac{1}{d}}\sim_{\,d,\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}}\textup{diam}(S)\leq C_{2}\,2^{-\frac{\ell(S)}{d}}.

This paragraph at the beginning of
[
Ste08
, Sect. 4]
, together with
[
Ste08
, Thm. 2.1 and the explanations given below]
, make it clear that
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
only depend on
d,\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}
, and
\mu_{\bm{x}}(\mathcal{P}_{0})
.
∎

Now we can show the corresponding result for the
ATOMIC_SPLIT
method.

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
I\times S
be created from an element
I_{0}\times S_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
by finitely many applications of the method
\textup{ATOMIC\_SPLIT}(\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
. Then

\displaystyle 2^{-\max\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\frac{\ell(I\times S)}{d}}\lesssim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}\textup{diam}(I\times S)\lesssim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}2^{-\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\frac{\ell(I\times S)}{d}}.

Proof.

Due to the last comment in
3.1
, the method generates simplices with
\ell(I)\sim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2}}\ell(I\times S)
and
\ell(S)=\ell(I\times S)
. Therefore, together with
3.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\textup{diam}(I\times S)

\displaystyle=\left(\textup{diam}(I)^{2}+\textup{diam}(S)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\sim_{\,d,\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}\left(2^{-2\ell(I)}+2^{-\frac{2\ell(S)}{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim 2^{-\min\left(\ell(I),\frac{\ell(S)}{d}\right)}\sim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2}}2^{-\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\frac{\ell(I\times S)}{d}}.

The derivation of the lower bound works similarly.
∎

3.2
Refinement of patches preserving mesh regularity

As mentioned above, our atomic refinement method preserves the shape regularity of the prisms on the level of an individual prism. Nonetheless, arbitrary atomic refinements of prisms in a space-time partition could lead to other undesirable results. Therefore, we have to construct an algorithm that refines a given element and a certain number of surrounding elements in order to maintain desired properties. Our goal is to create partitions that guarantee

(i)

conformity in space
:
\mathcal{T}(t,\mathcal{P})
is conforming for every
t\in[0,T]
,

(ii)

the validity of a
1
-irregular rule
in time. This means that if two elements of
\mathcal{P}
, let us call them
I\times S
and
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}
, touch in time, i.e., their closures share a
d
-dimensional hyperface in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
and
\#(\overline{I}\cap\overline{I^{\prime}})=1
, then their levels differ by at most one.

Remark 3.5
.

If
d=1
, the conformity condition in space is always fulfilled. To avoid arbitrary differences of levels of elements that touch in space, we instead require a
1
-irregular rule in space in this case as well.

In order to create such partitions, we adapt the idea of the refinement strategy for spatial triangulations presented in
[
Ste08
, Sect. 4-5]
that preserves initial conformity to our needs.

If we want to refine an element of a space-time partition, we have to consider the elements surrounding the element that have to be refined as well in order to maintain conformity in space and the 1-irregular rule in time (and space if
d=1
). This leads to the observation that whenever we want to refine an element of
\mathcal{P}
we have to consider all the elements that have to be refined as well in order to not violate our desired properties.

Therefore, in the case
d\geq 2
, let
\textup{RE}(S)
be the
refinement edge
of
S
, i.e., the edge of
S
that gets bisected in case of an application of
\textup{BISECT}(d,S)
, for
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
. For the sake of notation, we will put
\textup{RE}(S):=\emptyset
in case of
d=1
.

Definition 3.6
.

We define the set of
necessary refinements of neighbors
of
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P}):=\mathcal{N}_{t}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})\cup\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P}).

Here,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{t}(I\times S,\mathcal{P}):=\left\{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{I\times S\}\mid\#\left(\overline{I}\cap\overline{I^{\prime}}\right)=1,\ \textup{dim}(S\cap S^{\prime})=d,\ \text{and}\ \ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=\ell(I\times S)-1\right\}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle:=\left\{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{I\times S\}\mid|I\cap I^{\prime}|>0,\ 1\leq\textup{dim}(S\cap S^{\prime})\leq d-1,\ \text{and}\ \textup{RE}(S)\subset S^{\prime}\right\},\text{ if }d\geq 2,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})

\displaystyle:=\left\{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{I\times S\}\mid|I\cap I^{\prime}|>0,\ \#(S\cap S^{\prime})=1,\ \text{and}\ \ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=\ell(I\times S)-1\right\},\text{ if $d=1$}.

Remark 3.7
.

Note that
\mathcal{N}_{t}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
and
\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
are always disjoint sets.

If the space-time partition fulfills the mentioned regularity conditions, the set of necessary refinements can be characterized even further. The following lemma extends the result from
[
Ste08
, Cor. 4.6]
to a time-dependent setting.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{0}
satisfy the assumptions stated at the beginning of
Section

3
and assume that

\mathcal{P}
is a partition obtained from
\mathcal{P}_{0}
by a finite number of applications of
\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}
and it is conforming in space, additionally
1
-irregular in space if
d=1
, and
1
-irregular in time. Then for
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
, the prism
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
fulfills exactly one of the following properties:

•

\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=\ell(I\times S)
,
I=I^{\prime}
, and
\textup{RE}(S)=\textup{RE}(S^{\prime})
.

•

\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=\ell(I\times S)-1
,
I\subset I^{\prime}
, and there exists

I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}\in\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},d,s_{1},s_{2})

with
\textup{RE}(S)=\textup{RE}(S^{\prime\prime})
and
I=I^{\prime\prime}
.

Further, if
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{t}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
, then additionally
S\in\textup{BISECT}(d,S^{\prime})
is fulfilled.

Illustration of the two possible cases for
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
for
d=2
and
s_{2}=4s_{1}=2s_{1}d
.

Illustration of
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{t}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
for
d=2
and
s_{2}=2s_{1}=s_{1}d
.

Proof.

First, we consider the case of
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
. Let
t_{0}\in I\cap I^{\prime}
. Then
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(t_{0},\mathcal{P})
with RE
(S)\subset S^{\prime}
. Thus
[
Ste08
, Cor. 4.6]
for
d\geq 2
and the
1
-irregularity in space for
d=1
, respectively, imply that either

\displaystyle\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=\ell(S^{\prime})=\ell(S)=\ell(I\times S)\quad\text{or}\quad\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=\ell(S^{\prime})=\ell(S)-1=\ell(I\times S)-1.

Since
|I\cap I^{\prime}|>0
, and
\mathcal{P}
has been derived from
\mathcal{P}_{0}
, which has tensor product structure, by finitely many applications of
\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
, we know that there is
I_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
such that
I\in\textup{BISECT}(1,I_{0})^{\otimes\ell(I)}
and
I^{\prime}\in\textup{BISECT}(1,I_{0})^{\otimes\ell(I^{\prime})}
. On the one hand, assume
\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=\ell(I\times S)
, which yields
\ell(I^{\prime})=\ell(I)
and, in particular, implies
I=I^{\prime}
. Furthermore, in this case
\ell(S)=\ell(S^{\prime})
and thus
[
Ste08
, Cor. 4.6]
shows
\textup{RE}(S)=\textup{RE}(S^{\prime})
. Whereas on the other hand consider
\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=\ell(I\times S)-1
, which gives
\ell(I^{\prime})\leq\ell(I)
, and thus leads to
I\subset I^{\prime}
. Further, for any
I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}\in\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},d,s_{1},s_{2})
,
\ell(I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime})=\ell(I\times S)
, i.e.,
\ell(I^{\prime\prime})=\ell(I)
and
I,I^{\prime\prime}\in\textup{BISECT}(1,I^{\prime})^{\otimes\ell(I)-\ell(I^{\prime})}
. Among those
I^{\prime\prime}
, there must be one with
I=I^{\prime\prime}
. Further, in this case
\ell(S^{\prime})=\ell(S)-1
holds true, which implies the existence of
S^{\prime\prime}\in\textup{BISECT}(d,S^{\prime})
with
\textup{RE}(S)=\textup{RE}(S^{\prime\prime})
according to
[
Ste08
, Cor. 4.6]
, since
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(t_{0},\mathcal{P})
.

Lastly, we will consider
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{t}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
. The initial tensor product structure and
\ell(S^{\prime})=\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=\ell(I\times S)-1=\ell(S)-1
imply the asserted
S\in\textup{BISECT}(d,S^{\prime})
. Thus, the proof is complete.

∎

Corollary 3.9
.

Under the assumptions of the above lemma, it holds that,
\#\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})\lesssim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}}1
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the above lemma combined with
\kappa_{\mathcal{P}}\sim_{\,d}\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}
due to
[
MSS26
, Lem. 4.1]
.
∎

Now we propose a recursive refinement strategy, that exploits the results of
3.8
. This algorithm follows the idea of the
refine
-algorithm proposed in the beginning of
[
Ste08
, Sect. 5]
.

Algorithm 2

\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P},I\times S,d,s_{1},s_{2})

K\leftarrow\emptyset
% elements of
\mathcal{P}
that should be refined in the end

F\leftarrow\{I\times S\}
% elements that have to be considered for refinement to ensure desired properties

while

F\neq\emptyset

do

F_{new}\leftarrow\emptyset

for

I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in F

do

for

I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},\mathcal{P})\setminus(K\cup F)

do

if

\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=\ell(I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime})

then

F_{new}\leftarrow F_{new}\cup\{I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}\}

else

\mathcal{P}_{old}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}

\mathcal{P}\leftarrow\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P},I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime},d,s_{1},s_{2})

if

I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{old})

then

for

I^{\prime\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime},d,s_{1},s_{2}
)

do

if

I^{\prime\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},\mathcal{P})

then

F_{new}\leftarrow F_{new}\cup\{I^{\prime\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime\prime}\}

end

if

end

for

end

if

end

if

end

for

end

for

K\leftarrow K\cup F

F\leftarrow F_{new}

end

while

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}\setminus\{I\times S\}

for

\tilde{I}\times\tilde{S}\in K

do

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\cup\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(\tilde{I}\times\tilde{S},d,s_{1},s_{2})

end

for

return

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
fulfill the requirements from
3.8
and
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
. Then, the space-time partition
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}:=\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P},I\times S,d,s_{1},s_{2})
does as well and the minimum amount of applications of
\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
has been used in order to atomically refine
I\times S
and simultaneously maintain these properties. In particular, the algorithm terminates. Further, any newly created element has a level less or equal to
\ell(I\times S)+1
.

Remark 3.11
.

This result is the analogous to
[
Ste08
, Thm. 5.1]
, but for space-time prismatic meshes.

Proof.

We will prove this by induction over
\ell(I\times S)
. In general, we will denote all the sets in the algorithm with index
k\in\mathbb{N}
to indicate that they are created in the
k
-th loop of the
while
-procedure. (In fact, only
k=1,2
will be necessary.)

First, consider the case
\ell(I\times S)=0
. Then
I_{1}^{\prime}\times S_{1}^{\prime}=I\times S
and
I_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{1}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
.
3.8
now tells us that
\ell(I^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times S^{\prime\prime}_{1})=\ell(I\times S)=0
, since the other case
\ell(I^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times S^{\prime\prime}_{1})=\ell(I\times S)-1=-1
is impossible. Thus, the
else
-clause is never triggered in this loop and it terminates due to the boundedness of
\#\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
, as stated in
3.9
, with
K_{1}=\{I\times S\}
and
F_{1}=\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})=\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
.

For
d=1
, this set is already empty, for
d=2
it might be if
S
is at the boundary of
\Omega
. Nevertheless, we will proceed as if a second loop is always entered, without update of
K
and
F
, so we can avoid to distinguish cases. In the second loop (we may now assume
d\geq 2
temporarily),
I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime}\in F_{1}=\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
which in particular implies
\ell(I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime})=0
as above. Furthermore,
3.8
now even guarantees that
I_{2}^{\prime}=I
and
\textup{RE}(S_{2}^{\prime})=\textup{RE}(S)
. We will show that no
I_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime}
can be chosen, since
\mathcal{N}(I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{P})\setminus(K_{1}\cup F_{1})=\emptyset
. Suppose
I_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{P})\setminus(K_{1}\cup F_{1})
exists. Similar to before,
3.8
implies
I_{2}^{\prime\prime}=I_{2}^{\prime}=I
and
\textup{RE}(S_{2}^{\prime\prime})=\textup{RE}(S_{2}^{\prime})=\textup{RE}(S)
, therefore
I_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})\subset K_{1}\cup F_{1}
, which is a contradiction. Thus, the loop terminates, again due to
3.9
, with
K_{2}=\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})\cup\{I\times S\}
and
F_{2}=0
, therefore the whole
while
-clause, and thus the whole algorithm, terminates. Finally for any
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
K=K_{2}=\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})\cup\{I\times S\}
and for all
\tilde{I}\times\tilde{S}\in K
,
\tilde{I}=I
and
\textup{RE}(\tilde{S})=\textup{RE}\left(S\right)
hold true and
\mathcal{N}(\tilde{I}\times\tilde{S},\mathcal{P})\subset K
. Therefore, conformity in space as well as 1-regularity in time (and space if
d=1
), are fulfilled by
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. During the procedure, every newly created prism has been created from an element of
K
by
\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
, and all the elements of
K
are of level
\ell(I\times S)=0
, thus newly created elements are all of level
\ell(I\times S)+1=1
as claimed.

In fact, the situation
\ell(I\times S)=n\in\mathbb{N}
works very similar, where we assume that the assertion has already been shown for
n-1
. Then again,
I_{1}^{\prime}\times S_{1}^{\prime}=I\times S
and
I_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{1}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})
. Without loss of generality, we first consider all the cases, where
\ell(I_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{1}^{\prime\prime})\neq\ell(I_{1}^{\prime}\times S_{1}^{\prime})=\ell(I\times S)
, i.e, the situation where the
else
-case is entered. Then, in particular
\ell(I_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{1}^{\prime\prime})=\ell(I\times S)-1=n-1
due to
3.8
, therefore by the induction assumption,
\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P},\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
terminates with a space-time partition that fulfills the required grid properties. The space-time partition that has been created after all these calls of the content of the
else
-clause, will be called
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. According to
3.8
, some newly created elements in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
now share the level, time-interval, and refinement edge of
I\times S
(if
d\geq 2
), therefore they are added to
F_{new,1}
to avoid non-conformity in space in the final space-time partition
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
. Now, the elements
I_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{1}^{\prime\prime}
with
\ell(I_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{1}^{\prime\prime})=\ell(I\times S)
, which are in
\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P})\cap\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P}_{1})
, in particular, are considered. According to
3.8
, they also share interval and refinement edge with
I\times S
, such that the first
while
-loop terminates with
K_{1}=\{I\times S\}
and
F_{1}=\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P}_{1})=\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P}_{1})
. In particular, note that a call of the atomic refinement routine in the
else
-case only refines elements of order strictly less than
n=\ell(I\times S)
. Therefore, none of those elements that have been added to
F_{new,1}
by the
if
-case have already been refined by the possibly recursive calls of the atomic refinement routine in the
else
-case.

Again, there are cases, where the algorithm now directly terminates, for example if
d=1
. For technical reasons, we will again always assume that a second loop is entered (where applicable without update of
K
and
F
). Nevertheless, we can now temporarily assume
d\geq 2
without loss of generality. Now the second loop requires the choice of
I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(I\times S,\mathcal{P}_{1})
which implies
\ell(I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime})=\ell(I\times S)
by the construction of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and therefore
I_{2}^{\prime}=I
and
\textup{RE}(S_{2}^{\prime})=\textup{RE}(S)
due to
3.8
. Further,
I_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{1})\setminus(F_{1}\cup K_{1})
has to be chosen. Since,
I_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{1})
implies
0<|I_{2}^{\prime\prime}\cap I_{2}^{\prime}|=|I_{2}^{\prime\prime}\cap I|
and
S_{2}^{\prime\prime}\supset\textup{RE}(S_{2}^{\prime})=\textup{RE}(S)
, which yields
I_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S,\mathcal{P}_{1})\subset K_{1}\cup F_{1}
, we know that necessarily
I_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{t}(I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{1})
. As above, these elements have
\ell(I_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime\prime})=\ell(I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime})-1=\ell(I\times S)-1=n-1
, therefore
\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P},\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
preserves the required conditions by induction assumption. After all these calls of the mentioned method in the
else
-case, which is always triggered, we obtain a space-time partition
\mathcal{P}_{2}
fulfilling the required properties and
\mathcal{N}(I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{2})\subset K_{1}\cup F_{1}
for all
I_{2}^{\prime}\times S_{2}^{\prime}\in F_{1}\cup K_{1}
. Since the second loop now also terminates with
K_{2}=F_{1}\cup K_{1}
and
F_{2}=\emptyset
, the
while
-clause terminates, and thus the algorithm. Due to the above mentioned property, the last update step for
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
guarantees exactly the required properties. In this case, every new element has been either created by a call of
\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\cdot,I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime},d,s_{1},s_{2})
to some
I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}
with
\ell(I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime})=\ell(I\times S)-1
, which inductively only creates elements of level at most
\ell(I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime})+1=\ell(I\times S)
, or from an element of
K
by
\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
which, as in the case
n=0
, implies that they have a level of
\ell(I\times S)+1
.

The minimality property is clearly fulfilled due to the construction of the algorithm, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

During the course of this work, we found that illustrations play an important role in developing an intuitive understanding of how the algorithm
\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P},I\times S,d,s_{1},s_{2})
operates in different situations. We therefore present several corresponding sketches, which we hope will also be helpful to the reader. In particular, we distinguish between the cases
d\geq 2
and
d=1
.

Case
d\geq 2
:

Case
d=1
:

Lemma 3.13
.

Meshes
\mathcal{P}
created from
\mathcal{P}_{0}
by iterative applications of the patch refinement method presented above will also fulfill the following properties:

(i)

Hierarchical structure: For any
I\times S,I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
, either
I\times S=I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}
,
\left(\overline{I\times S}\right)\cap\left(\overline{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}}\right)=\emptyset
, or
\left(\overline{I\times S}\right)\cap\left(\overline{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}}\right)
is an
r
-dimensional face of
I\times S

or

I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}
with
r\in\{0,\dots,d\}
.
7
7

7

This is (only!) semantically very similar to conformity, where the
or
would become a
and
.

(ii)

Local grading: For
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
, we define the
neighborhood of
I\times S
, via

\displaystyle\omega^{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S):=\omega_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S):=\{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\mid\left(\overline{I\times S}\right)\cap\left(\overline{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}}\right)\neq\emptyset\}.

Further, we define the
extended neighborhood of degree
j
of
I\times S
inductively via

\displaystyle\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S):=\{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\mid I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\omega_{\mathcal{P}}(I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}),\>I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}\in\omega^{j-1}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)\},\quad j\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}.

Then
|I\times S|\sim_{\,j,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}|\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)|:=\Big|\bigcup\limits_{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)}I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\Big|
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
. Additionally, for such
I\times S
and
j
,
|I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}|\sim_{\,j,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}|I\times S|
for any
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)
. Further, for such
j
and
I\times S
, we define the
\widetilde{\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}}(I\times S)
, the
cylindric closure of the (extended) neighborhood of
I\times S
, via

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}}(I\times S):=\bigg\{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\>\Bigg|\>I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\subset\Big(\bigcup\limits_{I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}\in\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)}I^{\prime\prime}\Big)\times\Big(\bigcup\limits_{I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}\in\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)}S^{\prime\prime}\Big)\bigg\},\quad j\in\mathbb{N}.

Similarly, we observe
|I\times S|\sim_{\,j,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}|\widetilde{\omega_{\mathcal{P}}^{j}}(I\times S)|
, where the latter expression is defined correspondingly to
|\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)|
. In particular, we employ the notation

\widetilde{\omega_{\mathcal{P}}}(I\times S):=\widetilde{\omega^{1}_{\mathcal{P}}}(I\times S)
.
8
8

8

Note that we use
\omega
for both the smoothness moduli and the (extended) neighborhood domains, as this corresponds to the usual notation. However, the subscript and the context always clearly indicate which object is meant.

(iii)

For every
j\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds

\displaystyle\#\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)\lesssim_{\,j,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}}1.

For
j=1
, this in particular implies that the closure of every simplex in
\mathcal{P}
only intersects a uniformly bounded number of other elements of
\mathcal{P}
with its boundary.

Proof.

(i)
and
(iii)
are consequences of conformity in space and the
1
-irregular rule in time together with the conservation of the shape regularity of the partition by the atomic refinement as stated in
[
MSS26
, Lem. 4.1]
. Now we go over to prove
(ii)
for given
j\in\mathbb{N}
. The
1
-irregularity in time (and space if
d=1
) and conformity in space of
\mathcal{P}
together with the preservation of the minimum head angle, i.e.,
\kappa_{\mathcal{P}}\lesssim_{\,d}\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}
according to
3.1
6
, imply that there is
l=l(j,d,\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}})
such that
\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})\geq\ell(I\times S)-l
for any
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
and
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)
. In particular, this is a consequence of the structure of
\mathcal{N}_{\bm{x}}(I\times S)
shown in
3.8
, and the fact that after
d
spatial bisections, all the edges of a
d
-dimensional simplex have been bisected once as stated in
[
NSV09
, Problem 20]
. This directly yields
|I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}|\sim_{\,j,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}|I\times S|
and now allows us to calculate

\displaystyle|\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)|\leq\left|\widetilde{\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}}(I\times S)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left[\left(\sup\limits_{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)}|I^{\prime}|\right)\cdot\#\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)\right]\cdot\left[\left(\sup\limits_{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)}|S^{\prime}|\right)\cdot\#\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\,j,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}}\left(\sup\limits_{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)}|I^{\prime}|\right)\cdot\left(\sup\limits_{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)}|S^{\prime}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{0})}{\mu_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{0})}\cdot 2^{\left\lceil l\cdot\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}d}\right\rceil}|I|\cdot\frac{\mu_{3}(\mathcal{P}_{0})}{\mu_{4}(\mathcal{P}_{0})}\cdot 2^{l}|S|\lesssim_{\,j,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}|I\times S|,

with the help of
(iii)
. The opposite direction follows easily from
\left|\widetilde{\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}}(I\times S)\right|\geq|\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)|\geq|I\times S|
.
∎

In particular, similar properties have shown to be useful to define projection operators on finite element spaces in
[
SS23
, Sect. 6]
.

Remark 3.14
.

It is worth observing the following facts:

•

For the finite element analysis on appropriately refined meshes in the following sections, we would like to employ the Whitney-type estimate from
[
MSS26
, Thm. 1.2]
to domains of the type
\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}(I\times S)
,
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
. But this is in general not possible, since it might not be a cylindrical space-time domain. Therefore, we use the above defined
\widetilde{\omega^{j}_{\mathcal{P}}}(I\times S)
to work around this problem.

•

In the temporal direction, non-conformity, i.e., hanging-nodes, cannot be avoided unless one refines the whole space-time partition uniformly.

•

A bound on the cardinality of
\#\widetilde{\omega_{\mathcal{P}}}(I\times S)
,
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
, cannot be given for
1
-irregular meshes in time (and space if
d=1
) that are conforming in time. As an example look at
d=1
,
T=1
,
\Omega=[0,1]
,
\mathcal{P}_{0}:=\left\{[0,1]^{2}\right\}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{k}:=\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}\left(\mathcal{P}_{k-1},\left[1-2^{-k},1\right]^{2},1,1,1\right)
for
k\in\mathbb{N}.
Then
\#\widetilde{\omega_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}}\left([0,\frac{1}{2}]^{2}\right)=\#\mathcal{P}_{k}=3k+1
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and therefore
\#\widetilde{\omega_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}}\left([0,\frac{1}{2}]^{2}\right)\xrightarrow{k\rightarrow\infty}\infty
. Below is an illustration of this particular mesh refinement and its consequences.

The shaded part corresponds to
\omega_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}([0,\frac{1}{2}]^{2})
. Its cardinality is bounded in contrast to
\#\widetilde{\omega_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}}([0,\frac{1}{2}]^{2})
.

3.3
Algorithmic complexity

We will proceed similar to
[
Ste08
, Thm. 5.2 and Sect. 6]
. In this section we want to answer how large a space-time partition can get, if we apply the following (potentially non-terminating) algorithm that recursively refines certain marked elements of a partition with
\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\cdot,\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
until no elements are marked anymore.

Algorithm 3

\textup{MARKED}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P}_{0},d,s_{1},s_{2})

while
True
do

\mathcal{M}\leftarrow\textup{MARK}(\mathcal{P})

if

\mathcal{M}=\emptyset

then

break

end

if

for

I\times S\in\mathcal{M}

do

if

I\times S\in\mathcal{P}

then
% if not, it has already been refined in a previous loop of this
for
-clause

\mathcal{P}\leftarrow\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P},I\times S,d,s_{1},s_{2})

end

if

end

for

end

while

We will denote the space-time partition at the end of the
k
-th loop of the
while
-clause by
\mathcal{P}_{k}
and the set of marked elements in
\mathcal{P}_{k}
as
\mathcal{M}_{k}
. The goal of this section is to show

Theorem 3.15
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then it holds

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{P}_{k}-\#\mathcal{P}_{0}\lesssim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\#\mathcal{M}_{i-1}.

In order to prove this theorem, we will need a lemma that bounds the distance of an element
I\times S\in\mathcal{P}
to an element
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
that has been newly created by a call of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}:=\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P},I\times S,d,s_{1},s_{2})
. Here, we assume
\mathcal{P}
to fulfill the usual properties from

3.8
, i.e., conformity in space as well as 1-irregularity in time (and space, if
d=1
). In the stationary setting this corresponds to
[
Ste08
, Thm. 5.2]
, the proof of which we have modified to suit our setting.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
I\times S
,
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{P}
, and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be as above. Then there exists a constant
C
only depending on
d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}
, and
\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})
such that

\displaystyle d(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},I\times S)\leq C\,2^{\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}\sum\limits_{k=\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})}^{\ell(I\times S)}2^{-\frac{k}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}.

Here,
C
is the constant from
3.4
corresponding to the upper bound.

Proof.

We will prove the assertion by induction over
\ell(I\times S)
.
9
9

9

This proof relies heavily on the one of
3.10
, therefore, we will use the results found there without explicit mentioning it.
If
\ell(I\times S)=0
, then
\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=1
and
I\times S
touches
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}
in space, i.e.,
d(I\times S,I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=0
, which corresponds to the assertion. In particular, if
d=1
, then
\ell(I\times S)=0
even implies that
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(I\times S,d,s_{1},s_{2})
. Now assume the lemma holds true for levels strictly less than
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
\ell(I\times S)=l
. Then there are two possibilities. If
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}
has been created by the application of
\textup{ATOMIC}\_\textup{SPLIT}(\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
to an element of the set
K
from the algorithm, then again
d(I\times S,I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=0
and nothing is left to be shown. Otherwise,
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}
is created by a recursive call of
\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P},I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime},d,s_{1},s_{2})
with
\ell(I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime})=\ell(I\times S)-1=l-1
. Then the fact that
(\overline{I\times S})\cap(\overline{I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}})\neq\emptyset
, together with the induction assumption and
3.4
now shows

\displaystyle d(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},I\times S)

\displaystyle\leq d(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime})+\textup{diam}(I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\leq C\,2^{\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}\sum\limits_{k=\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})}^{\ell(I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime})}2^{-\frac{k}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}+C\,2^{-\frac{\ell(I^{\prime\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime})}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}

\displaystyle=C\,2^{\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}\sum\limits_{k=\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})}^{\ell(I\times S)}2^{-\frac{k}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}.\qed

Now we can go over to the proof of
3.15
which is very similar to the one of the time-independent analogue from
[
Ste08
, Thm. 6.1]
. The latter itself is based on the proof of
[
BDD04
, Thm. 2.4]
.

Proof of
3.15
.

First, we define the positive sequences
a:\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{-1\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
a(p):=(p+2)^{-2}
and
b:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
b(p):=2^{\frac{p}{d+1}\cdot\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}
. Those have the following properties, which can be shown with elementary techniques from calculus:

(i)

\sum\limits_{p=-1}^{\infty}a(p):=C_{1}<\infty
,

(ii)

\sum\limits_{p=0}^{\infty}b(p)2^{-\frac{p}{d}\cdot\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}:=C_{2}(d,s_{1},s_{2})<\infty
, and

(iii)

\inf\limits_{p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}a(p)b(p):=C_{3}(d,s_{1},s_{2})>0
.

We will need the constant
A:=C\left(\frac{2^{\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}}{1-2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}}+1\right)C_{2}
, where
C
is the constant from the upper bound of
3.4
and
3.16
. Moreover we shall need the properties
(i)
-
(iii)
throughout the proof. Now let
k\in\mathbb{N}
(for
k=0
there is nothing to be shown),
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
any intermediate partition between
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}_{k}
during the call of
\textup{MARKED}\_\textup{REFINE}(\mathcal{P}_{0},d,s_{1},s_{2})
, and
\mathcal{M}\subset\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{M}_{i-1}
its subset of marked elements for which the subroutine
\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\tilde{\mathcal{P}},\cdot,d,s_{1},s_{2})
has been called. Further, we define the following function
\lambda:\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
via

\displaystyle\lambda(I^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times S^{\prime},I\times S)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}a(\ell(I\times S)-\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})),&\text{if }d(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},I\times S)<A\,2^{-\frac{\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}{d}\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})}\text{ and }\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})\leq\ell(I\times S)+1,\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Now let
I\times S\in\mathcal{M}
and
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
l\leq\ell(I\times S)+1
. Due to
3.4
, there are only finitely many
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
with
\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=l
such that
d(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},I\times S)\leq A\,2^{-\frac{\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}{d}l}
, whereas the number is uniformly bounded by
d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}
, and
\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}}\lambda(I^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times S^{\prime},I\times S)=\sum\limits_{l=0}^{\ell(I\times S)+1}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P},\\
\ell(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})=l\end{subarray}}\lambda(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},I\times S)\lesssim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}\sum\limits_{l=-1}^{\ell(I\times S)}a(l)\leq\sum\limits_{l=-1}^{\infty}a(l)\sim 1,

where we have applied
(i)
from the beginning of the proof in the final step. This yields

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{M}}\sum\limits_{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}}\lambda(I^{\prime}

\displaystyle\times S^{\prime},I\times S)\lesssim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}\sum\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{M}}1=\#\mathcal{M}.

(5)

Next, we will consider
I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus(\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{P}_{0})
and the chain of prisms
(I_{0}\times S_{0},I_{1}\times S_{1},\dots,I_{e}\times S_{e})
,
e\in\mathbb{N}
,
I_{0}\times S_{0}:=I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}
, such that
I_{e}\times S_{e}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
, and
I_{i-1}\times S_{i-1}
has been created during a call of the method
\textup{PATCH}\_\textup{REFINE}(\tilde{\mathcal{P}},I_{i}\times S_{i},d,s_{1},s_{2})
,
i=1,\dots,e
. We know that
\ell(I_{i-1}\times S_{i-1})\leq\ell(I_{i}\times S_{i})+1
,
i=1,\dots,e
, due to
3.10
. Therefore, it exists
\nu\in\{1,\dots,e\}
minimal with
\ell(I_{\nu}\times S_{\nu})=\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0})-1
. In particular, this implies that
\ell(I_{i}\times S_{i})>\ell(I_{\nu}\times S_{\nu})=\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0})-1
for
i\in\{0,\dots,\nu-1\}
.

Now let
j\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
. Due to
3.4
and
3.16
, we obtain

\displaystyle d(I_{0}\times S_{0},I_{j}\times S_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits^{j}_{i=1}d(I_{i-1}\times S_{i-1},I_{i}\times S_{i})+\sum\limits^{j-1}_{i=1}\textup{diam}(I_{i}\times S_{i})

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j}2^{\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}\sum\limits_{k=\ell(I_{i-1}\times S_{i-1})}^{\ell(I_{i}\times S_{i})}2^{-\frac{k}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{j-1}2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\ell(I_{i}\times S_{i})}\right).

Since for all
i\in\{1,...,j\}
,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=\ell(I_{i-1}\times S_{i-1})}^{\ell(I_{i}\times S_{i})}2^{-\frac{k}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}<2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\ell(I_{i-1}\times S_{i-1})}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-\frac{k}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}=\frac{2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\ell(I_{i-1}\times S_{i-1})}}{1-2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}},

due to the well-known formula for a geometric sum with respect to
2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}<1
, we can further estimate

\displaystyle d(I_{0}\times S_{0},I_{j}\times S_{j})

\displaystyle<C\left(\frac{2^{\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}}{1-2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}}+1\right)\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j-1}2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\ell(I_{i}\times S_{i})}

\displaystyle=C\left(\frac{2^{\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}}{1-2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}}+1\right)\sum\limits_{p=0}^{\infty}m(p,j)2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\left(\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0})+p\right)},

where
m(p,j):=\#\{I_{i}\times S_{i}\mid\ell(I_{i}\times S_{i})=\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0})+p,\ i=0,\dots,j-1\}
for any
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Now we have to distinguish two cases. If
m(p,\nu)\leq b(p)
for all
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then using
(ii)
and the definition of the constant
A
yields

\begin{split}d(I_{0}\times S_{0},I_{\nu}\times S_{\nu})&<C\left(\frac{2^{\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}}{1-2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}}+1\right)\left(\sum\limits_{p=0}^{\infty}b(p)2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)p}\right)2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0})}\\
&=C\left(\frac{2^{\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}}{1-2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)}}+1\right)C_{2}\,2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0})}=A\,2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0})}.\end{split}

(6)

and therefore, it holds

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{M}}\lambda(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},I\times S)\geq\lambda(I_{0}\times S_{0},I_{\nu}\times S_{\nu})=a(\ell(I_{\nu}\times S_{\nu})-\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0}))=a(-1)=1\gtrsim 1.

(7)

Otherwise, there exists one
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
m(p,\nu)>b(p)
. Since the definition of
m(p,\cdot)
implies that it is monotonously increasing in the second component, there is
j=j(p)\in\{1,\dots,\nu\}
minimal such that
m(p,j(p))>b(p)
. Now let
p^{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be chosen such that
j^{*}:=j(p^{*})
is the minimal value of the sequence
(j(p))_{p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. In fact, it must hold
j^{*}-1\geq 1
, i.e.,
j^{*}\geq 2
, since
j^{*}=1
would lead to the contradiction
1=m(p^{*},1)>b(p^{*})\geq 1
. Therefore, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
m(p,j^{*}-1)\leq m(p,j(p)-1)\leq b(p)
, due to the monotonicity of
m(p,\cdot)
and the minimality condition imposed on
j(p)
.

This implies
d(I_{0}\times S_{0},I_{k}\times S_{k})<A\,2^{-\frac{1}{d}\min\left(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}},1\right)\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0})}
for any
k\in\{0,\dots,j^{*}-1\}
, as above in (
6
). In particular, for all such
k
with
\ell(I_{k}\times S_{k})=\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0})+p^{*}
, we can conclude that
\lambda(I_{0}\times S_{0},I_{k}\times S_{k})=a(p^{*})
. Then, similar to before, we can estimate

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{M}}\lambda(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},I\times S)

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,\dots,j^{*}-1\}\text{ with}\\
\ell(I_{k}\times S_{k})=\ell(I_{0}\times S_{0})+p^{*}\end{subarray}}\lambda(I_{0}\times S_{0},I_{k}\times S_{k})=m(p^{*},j^{*})a(p^{*})

(8)

\displaystyle>b(p^{*})a(p^{*})\geq\inf\limits_{p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}a(p)b(p)=C_{3}\gtrsim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2}}1,

where we have employed
(iii)
in the last step. Now we can finally prove the assertion, using first (
7
) and (
8
), respectively, and then (
5
):

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{P}-\#\mathcal{P}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\#(\mathcal{P}\setminus(\mathcal{P}_{0}\cap\mathcal{P}))\lesssim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2}}\sum\limits_{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{P}\setminus(\mathcal{P}_{0}\cap\mathcal{P}))}\sum\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{M}}\lambda(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},I\times S)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{I\times S\in\mathcal{M}}\sum\limits_{I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}}\lambda(I^{\prime}\times S^{\prime},I\times S)\lesssim_{\,d,s_{1},s_{2},\kappa_{\mathcal{P}_{0}},\mu(\mathcal{P}_{0})}\#\mathcal{M}\leq\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\#\mathcal{M}_{i-1}.\qed

4
Support of basis functions and quasi-interpolation

4.1
An investigation of the mesh geometry of hanging nodes

Let
\hat{I}:=[0,1]
,
\hat{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the
d
-dimensional standard simplex, and
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
. The
Lagrangian basis nodes
with respect to
\Pi^{r_{1}}(\hat{I})
and
\Pi^{r_{2}}(\hat{S})
, i.e., polynomials on
\hat{I}
and
\hat{S}
of order
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, respectively, are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{r_{1}}(\hat{I}):=\left\{\frac{n}{r_{1}-1}\bigg|\ n=0,\dots,r_{1}-1\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{L}_{r_{2}}(\hat{S}):=\left\{\frac{\alpha}{r_{2}-1}\bigg|\ \alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d},\,|\alpha|\leq r_{2}-1\right\}.

Further, let
I
be an interval and
S
a
d
-dimensional simplex. Now consider the corresponding bijective, affine transformations
\Phi_{I}:\hat{I}\rightarrow\overline{I}
and
\Phi_{S}:\hat{S}\rightarrow S
, that allow us to define the (isotropic) Lagrangian basis nodes with respect to
I
and
S
as follows:
